Spherical higher order Fourier analysis over finite fields II: additive combinatorics for shifted modules Thanks: The author was partially supported by the NSF Grant DMS-2247331
Wenbo Sun

Abstract
. This paper is the second part of the series Spherical higher order Fourier analysis over finite fields , aiming to develop the higher order Fourier analysis method along spheres over finite fields, and to solve the geometric Ramsey conjecture in the finite field setting. In this paper, we study additive combinatorial properties for shifted modules, i.e. the structure of sets of the form E ± E E\pm E , where E E is a collection of shifted modules of the polynomial ring ℝ ⁡ [ x 1 , … , x d ] \mathbb{R}[x_{1},\dots,x_{d}] and we identify two modules if their difference contains the zero polynomial. We show that under appropriate definitions, the set E ± E E\pm E enjoys properties similar to the conventional setting where E E is a subset of an abelian group. In particular, among other results, we prove the Balog-Gowers-Szemerédi theorem, the Rusza’s quasi triangle inequality and a weak form of the Plünnecke-Rusza theorem in the setting of shifted modules. We also show that for a special class of maps ξ \xi from ℤ K d \mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d} to the collection of all shifted modules of ℝ ⁡ [ x 1 , … , x d ] \mathbb{R}[x_{1},\dots,x_{d}] , if the set ξ ⁡ ( ℤ K d ) + ξ ⁡ ( ℤ K d ) \xi(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d})+\xi(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}) has large additive energy, then ξ \xi is an almost linear Freiman homomorphism. This result is the crucial additive combinatorial input we need to prove the spherical Gowers inverse theorem in later parts of the series.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
05C99, 05D99

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Additive combinatorics for affine subspaces and shifted modules

This paper is the second part of the series
Spherical higher order Fourier analysis over finite fields

[
7
,
8
,
9
]
.
The purpose of this paper is to provide the additive combinatorial results we need for the proof of the spherical Gowers inverse theorem in
[
8
]
.
Let
G
be a discrete abelian group. A central topic in additive combinatorics is to study the structure of subsets
E
of
G
with large additive energy (meaning that the set
\{(a,b,c,d)\in E^{4}\colon a+b=c+d\}
has cardinality
O(|E|^{3})
) or with small doubling constant (meaning that
|E+E|=O(|E|)
). Such sets are known to have structures related to arithmetic progressions. We refer the readers to
[
10
]
for more details.
Based on the existing knowledge in additive combinatorics, it was shown in Lemma F.1 of
[
4
]
that if a function
f\colon\{0,\dots,N-1\}\to\mathbb{T}
has large additive energy, then
f
must have an almost linear structure in the sense that

f(h)=C+\sum_{i=1}^{L}\{\alpha_{i}h\}\beta_{i}\mod\mathbb{Z}

for a large set of
h\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}
for some
L\in\mathbb{N}
and
C,\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
. This linearization result plays an important role in the proof of the Gowers inverse theorem in
[
4
]
.

In order to prove the spherical Gowers inverse theorem in
[
8
]
, we also need a linearization result in the spherical setting. In our context, the structure for functions with large additive energy is characterized by the following definition (recall that
\tau\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}\to\{0,\dots,p-1\}
and
\iota\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
are the natural embeddings):

Definition 1.1

(Almost linear function and Freiman homomorphism)
.

Let
L\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,

G
be an additive group,
H
be a subset of
G
,
R
be a subset of
\mathbb{R}
, and
\xi\colon H\to\mathbb{R}
be a map.
We say that
\xi
is an
R
-almost linear function
of
complexity
at most
L
if
there exist
\alpha_{i}\in\widehat{G}
(the Pontryagin dual of
G
, see Section
1.2
for the definition) and
\beta_{i}\in R
for all
1\leq i\leq L
such that for all
h\in H
,

\xi(h):=\sum_{i=1}^{L}\{\alpha_{i}\cdot h\}\beta_{i}.

We say that an
R
-almost linear function
\xi
is an
R
-almost linear Freiman homomorphism
if for all
h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4}\in H
with
h_{1}+h_{2}=h_{3}+h_{4}
, we have that

\xi(h_{1})+\xi(h_{2})\equiv\xi(h_{3})+\xi(h_{4})\mod\mathbb{Z}.

Let
p
be a prime.
We say that a map
\xi\colon H\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
is an
almost linear function/Freiman homomorphism
of complexity at most
L
if there exists a
\mathbb{Z}/p
-almost linear function/Freiman homomorphism
\xi^{\prime}
with
\xi^{\prime}(H)\subseteq\mathbb{Z}/p
of complexity at most
L
such that

\xi(h)=\iota(p\xi^{\prime}(h))
for all
h\in H
.

We say that a map
\xi\colon H\to\mathbb{R}[x_{1},\dots,x_{d}]
(resp.
\mathbb{F}_{p}[x_{1},\dots,x_{d}]
) is an
R
-almost linear function/
R
-Freiman homomorphism
(resp.
almost linear function/Freiman homomorphism
) of complexity at most
L
if
\xi_{m}
is an
R
-almost linear function/
R
-Freiman homomorphism (resp. almost linear function/Freiman homomorphism) of complexity at most
L
for all
m=(m_{1},\dots,m_{d})\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
, where
\xi_{m}(h)
is the coefficient of the monomial
x_{1}^{m_{1}}\dots x_{d}^{m_{d}}
of
\xi(h)
.

In order to prove the 3-step spherical Gowers inverse theorem, the following linearization result is needed in
[
8
]
.
1
1

1

The linearization result is not necessary for the proof of 2 or less step spherical Gowers inverse theorems.

Proposition 1.2

(Linearization result for affine supsaces)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
with
d\gg 1
,
\delta>0
and
p\gg_{\delta,d}1
be a prime. Let
H\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
with
|H|>\delta p^{d}
, and
\xi_{i}
be a map from
H
to
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
for
1\leq i\leq 4
.
Suppose that there exists

U\subseteq\{(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4})\in H^{4}\colon h_{1}+h_{2}=h_{3}+h_{4}\}

with
|U|>\delta|H|^{3}
such that for all
(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4})\in U
,

(1.1)

\xi_{1}(h_{1})+\xi_{2}(h_{2})-\xi_{3}(h_{3})-\xi_{4}(h_{4})\in\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}\}.

Then there exist
H^{\prime}\subseteq H
with
|H^{\prime}|\gg_{\delta,d}p^{d}
, some
g\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
, and a map
T\colon H^{\prime}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
whose projection to each of the
d
coordinates is an almost linear Freiman homomorphism of complexity
O_{\delta,d}(1)

such that

\xi_{1}(h)-T(h)-g\in\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{h\}\text{ for all $h\in H^{\prime}$.}

An alternative way to interpret Proposition
1.2
is as follows. For affine subspaces
V+c
and
V^{\prime}+c^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, we informally write
V+c\sim V^{\prime}+c^{\prime}
if
c-c^{\prime}\in V+V^{\prime}
(see Section
2.1
for the precise definitions).
Roughly speaking, Proposition
1.2
claims that if the maps

\tilde{\xi}_{i}\colon h\mapsto\xi_{i}(h)+\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{h\}

from
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
to at most one dimensional affine subspaces of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
has large additive energy in the sense
that

\tilde{\xi}_{1}(h_{1})+\tilde{\xi}_{2}(h_{2})\sim\tilde{\xi}_{3}(h_{3})+\tilde{\xi}_{4}(h_{4})

for all
(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4})\in U
, then
\tilde{\xi}_{i}(h)
equals to an almost linear Freiman homomorphism plus a constant modulo
\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{h\}
.

In order to study the higher-step spherical Gowers inverse theorem, we need to replace the functions
\xi_{i}
in Proposition
1.2
by functions taking values in the ring of polynomials.
Let
R
be a subset of
\mathbb{R}
,
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
s\in\mathbb{N}
.
We use
\text{HP}_{R,d}(s)
to denote the set of homogeneous polynomial in
\mathbb{R}[x_{1},\dots,x_{d}]
of degree
s
whose coefficients are taken from
R
.

Convention 1.3
.

In this paper, the zero polynomial is considered as a degree
s
polynomial for any
s\in\mathbb{Z}
. In particular, the zero polynomial belongs to
\text{HP}_{R,d}(s)
for any
s\in\mathbb{N}
.
For convenience, if
s<0
, then we use
\text{HP}_{R,d}(s)
to denote the set consisting only the zero polynomial.

In order to generalize (
1.1
) to
\text{HP}_{\mathbb{R},d}(s)
-valued functions, we also need to extend the concept of subspaces of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
accordingly:

Definition 1.4

(
M
-module for real polynomials)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+},p
be a prime and
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form associated with the matrix
A
(see Section
1.2
for definitions).
We say that a subset
I
of the polynomial ring
\mathbb{R}[x_{1},\dots,x_{d}]

is an
M
-module
if there exists a subspace
V
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
such that

f\in I\Leftrightarrow f(n)\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\text{ with }(\iota(n)A)\cdot\iota(n)=0\text{ and }(hA)\cdot\iota(n)=0\text{ for all }h\in V.

In this case we denote
I
as
J^{M}_{V}
. For convenience we denote

J^{M}:=J^{M}_{\{\mathbb{0}\}}\text{ and }J^{M}_{h_{1},\dots,h_{k}}:=J^{M}_{\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{h_{1},\dots,h_{k}\}}.

for all
h_{1},\dots,h_{k}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.

For convenience, in the rest of the paper, we denote

(1.2)

N(s):=(2s+16)(15s+453)\text{ for all }s\in\mathbb{Z}.

The following is the additive combinatorial property we need in order to prove the general spherical Gowers inverse theorem in
[
8
]
, which is also the main result of this paper.

Theorem 1.5

(Linearization result for shifted modules)
.

Let
d,K,r,s\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, with
d\geq N(s)
,
\delta>0
and
p\gg_{\delta,d,r}1
be a prime dividing
K
,

M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a non-degenerate quadratic form,
H\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}
with
|H|>\delta K^{d}
, and
\xi_{i}
be a map from
H
to
\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z}/p^{r},d}(s)
for
1\leq i\leq 4
.
Suppose that there exists

U\subseteq\{(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4})\in H^{4}\colon h_{1}+h_{2}=h_{3}+h_{4}\}

with
|U|>\delta|H|^{3}
such that for all
(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4})\in U
,

\xi_{1}(h_{1})+\xi_{2}(h_{2})-\xi_{3}(h_{3})-\xi_{4}(h_{4})\in J^{M}_{\iota(h_{1}),\iota(h_{2}),\iota(h_{3})}.

Then there exist
H^{\prime}\subseteq H
with
|H^{\prime}|\gg_{\delta,d,r}K^{d}
, some
g\in\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z}/p^{r},d}(s)
, and a
\mathbb{Z}/p^{r}
-almost linear Freiman homomorphism
T\colon H^{\prime}\to\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z}/p^{r},d}(s)
of complexity
O_{\delta,d}(1)
. such that

\xi_{1}(h)-T(h)-g\in J^{M}_{\iota(h)}\text{ for all $h\in H^{\prime}$.}

We will make essential use of Theorem
1.5
in the Furstenburg-Weiss type argument in
[
8
]
, which is an intermediate step towards the proof of the spherical Gowers inverse theorem. We refer the readers to Steps 2 and 3 of Section 1.2 of
[
8
]
for details.

Remark 1.6
.

Note that if
s=1,\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}=\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
M(n)=n\cdot n
, then there is a natural group homomorphism
\sigma
between
(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d},+)
and
(\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z}/p,d}(1),+)
given by
\sigma(h)(n):=\frac{1}{p}(n\cdot\tau(h))
, which maps
\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{h\}
to
J^{M}_{h}\cap\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z}/p,d}(1)
since all polynomials in
J^{M}_{h}\cap\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z}/p,d}(1)
are integer scalar multiples of
\sigma(h)
.
Moreover, for all
v\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and subspace
V
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, we have
v\in V
if and only if
\sigma(v)\in J^{M}_{V}
. So (
1.1
) is equivalent to

\xi^{\prime}_{1}(h_{1})+\xi^{\prime}_{2}(h_{2})-\xi^{\prime}_{3}(h_{3})-\xi^{\prime}_{4}(h_{4})\in J^{M}_{h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}},

where
\xi_{i}^{\prime}:=\sigma\circ\xi_{i}
is a map from
H
to
\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z}/p,d}(1)
.
Therefore, Proposition
1.2
is a special case of Theorem
1.5
when
s=1
.

While additive combinatorial properties for abelian groups have been studied extensively in literature, very little is known for additive combinatorial properties of affine subspaces or shifted modules. In particular, all the powerful tools in additive combinatoric are unavailable. In order to prove Theorem
1.5
, we need to establish foundational tools in analogous to the abelian group setting from scratch. An interesting feature of this paper is that we use many tools in graph theory to study additive combinatorial properties for shifted modules (especially in Section
5
).

Since the proof of Theorem
1.5
is very technical, when reading this paper for the first time, the readers are recommended to focus on the case
s=1
and
\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}=\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
throughout, which captures almost all the main idea of the proof of the general case. In this case
\xi_{i}
can be viewed as a map from
H
to
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, and
J^{M}_{h_{1},\dots,h_{k}}
can be interpreted as
\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{h_{1},\dots,h_{k}\}
(if
d\gg_{k}1
).

1.2.
Organizations and notations

In Section
2
, we define a non-transitive relation for shifted modules, and introduce some terminologies in graph theory which will be used later to prove Theorem
1.5
. We also provide an outline of the proof Theorem
1.5
with the help of these new languages.
In Section
3
, we prove an intersection property for
M
-modules, which is an approach that will be used frequently later in this paper to study the intersections of different
M
-modules sharing a common factor.
Sections
4
–
9
are devoted to the proof of Theorem
1.5
. We refer the readers to Section
2.3
for a more details explanation for the roles these sections play in the proof of Theorem
1.5
(after introducing all the relevant definitions). In Section
10
, we collect some open questions. In Appendix
A
, we recall some definitions and propositions from
[
7
]
which are used in this paper.

Below are the notations we use in this paper:

•

Let
\mathbb{N},\mathbb{N}_{+},\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}+,\mathbb{C}
denote the set of non-negative integers, positive integers, integers, rational numbers, real numbers, positive real numbers, and complex numbers, respectively. Denote
\mathbb{T}:=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
. Let
\mathbb{F}_{p}
denote the finite field with
p
elements. Let
\mathbb{Z}_{K}
denote the cyclic group with
K
elements.

•

Let
\mathbb{Z}/p^{\mathbb{N}}
denote the set of numbers of the form
a/p^{b}
for some
a\in\mathbb{Z}
and
b\in\mathbb{N}
.

•

Throughout this paper,
d
is a fixed positive integer,
p
is a prime number and
K
is a positive integer dividing
p
.

•

Throughout this paper, unless otherwise stated, all vectors are assumed to be horizontal vectors.

•

Throughout this paper, we use

\tau\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}\to\{0,\dots,p-1\}
to denote the natural bijective embedding, and use
\iota
to denote the map from
\mathbb{Z}
(or
\mathbb{Z}_{K}
for any
K
divisible by
p
) to
\mathbb{F}_{p}
given by
\iota(n):=\tau^{-1}(n\mod p\mathbb{Z})
.
We also use

\tau
to denote the map from
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
to
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
(or
\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{k}
) given by
\tau(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}):=(\tau(x_{1}),\dots,\tau(x_{k}))
,
and

\iota
to denote the map from
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
(or
\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{k}
) to
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
given by
\iota(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}):=(\iota(x_{1}),\dots,

\iota(x_{k}))
.
When there is no confusion, we will not state the domain and range of
\tau
and
\iota
explicitly.

•

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a collection of parameters and
A,B,c\in\mathbb{R}
. We write
A\gg_{\mathcal{C}}B
if
|A|\geq K|B|
and
A=O_{\mathcal{C}}(B)
if
|A|\leq K|B|
for some
K>0
depending only on the parameters in
\mathcal{C}
.
In the above definitions, we allow the set
\mathcal{C}
to be empty. In this case
K
will be a universal constant.

•

Let
[N]
denote the set
\{0,\dots,N-1\}
.

•

For
x\in\mathbb{R}
, let
\lfloor x\rfloor
denote the largest integer which is not larger than
x
, and
\lceil x\rceil
denote the smallest integer which is not smaller than
x
. Denote
\{x\}:=x-\lfloor x\rfloor
.

•

Let
X
be a finite set and
f\colon X\to\mathbb{C}
be a function. Denote
\mathbb{E}_{x\in X}f(x):=\frac{1}{|X|}\sum_{x\in X}f(x)
, the average of
f
on
X
.

•

For
F=\mathbb{Z}^{k}
or
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
, and
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}),y=(y_{1},\dots,y_{k})\in F
, let
x\cdot y\in\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{F}_{p}
denote the dot product given by

x\cdot y:=x_{1}y_{1}+\dots+x_{k}y_{k}.

•

We write affine subspaces of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
as
V+c
, where
V
is a subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
passing through
\mathbb{0}
, and
c\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.

•

For an additive group
G
, we use
\widehat{G}
to denote the
Pontryagin dual
of
G
, i.e. the set of all continuous group homomorphism from
G
to
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
. For
g\in G
and
h\in\widehat{G}
, we use
\{h\cdot g\}
to denote the representative of
h(g)
on the fundamental domain
[0,1)
of
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
.

•

For a polynomial
P\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
, let
V(P)
denote the set of
n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
such that
P(n)=0
. For a polynomial
P\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to\mathbb{R})
, let
V_{p}(P)
denote the set of
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
such that
P(n+pm)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
.

•

There is a natural correspondence between polynomials taking values in
\mathbb{F}_{p}
and polynomials taking values in
\mathbb{Z}/p
. Let
F\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d^{\prime}})
and
f\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to(\mathbb{Z}/p)^{d^{\prime}})
be polynomials of degree at most
s
for some
s<p
. If
F=\iota\circ pf\circ\tau
, then we say that
F
is
induced
by
f
and
f
is a
lifting
of
F
.
5
5

5

As is explained in
[
7
]
,
\iota\circ pf\circ\tau
is well defined.

We say that
f
is a
regular lifting
of
F
if in addition
f
has the same degree as
F
and
f
has
\{0,\frac{1}{p},\dots,\frac{p-1}{p}\}
-coefficients.

We also need to recall the notations regarding quadratic forms defined in
[
7
]
.

Definition 1.7
.

We say that a function
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
is a
quadratic form
if

M(n)=(nA)\cdot n+n\cdot u+v

for some
d\times d
symmetric matrix
A
in
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, some
u\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
v\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
.
We say that
A
is the matrix
associated to

M
.
We say that
M
is
pure
if
u=\mathbb{0}
.
We say that
M
is
homogeneous
if
u=\mathbb{0}
and
v=0
. We say that
M
is
non-degenerate
if
M
is of rank
d
, or equivalently,
\det(A)\neq 0
.

We use
\text{rank}(M):=\text{rank}(A)
to denote the
rank
of
M
.
Let
V+c
be an affine subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
of dimension
r
. There exists a (not necessarily unique) bijective linear transformation
\phi\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{r}\to V
.
We define the
rank

\text{rank}(M|_{V+c})
of
M
restricted to
V+c
as the rank of the quadratic form
M(\phi(\cdot)+c)
. It was proved in
[
7
]
that
\text{rank}(M|_{V+c})
is independent of the choice of
\phi
.

For any subspace
V
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, let
V^{\perp_{M}}
denote the set of
\{n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\colon(mA)\cdot n=0\text{ for all }m\in V\}
.

Quadratic forms can also be defined in the
\mathbb{Z}/p
-setting.

Definition 1.8
.

We say that a function
M\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p
is a
quadratic form
if

M(n)=\frac{1}{p}((nA)\cdot n+n\cdot u+v)

for some
d\times d
symmetric matrix
A
in
\mathbb{Z}
, some
u\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
v\in\mathbb{Z}
.
We say that
A
is the matrix
associated to

M
.

By Lemma A.1 of
[
7
]
,
any quadratic form
\tilde{M}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p
associated with the matrix
\tilde{A}
induces a quadratic form
M:=\iota\circ p\tilde{M}\circ\tau\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
associated with the matrix
\iota(\tilde{A})
. Conversely, any quadratic form
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
associated with the matrix
A
admits a regular lifting
\tilde{M}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p
, which is a quadratic form associated with the matrix
\tau(A)
.

For a quadratic form
\tilde{M}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p
, we say that
\tilde{M}
is
pure/homogeneous/
p
-non-degenerate
if the quadratic form
M:=\iota\circ p\tilde{M}\circ\tau
induced by
\tilde{M}
is pure/homogeneous/non-degenerate.
The
p
-rank
of
\tilde{M}
, denoted by
\text{rank}_{p}(\tilde{M})
, is defined to be the rank of
M
.

We say that
h_{1},\dots,h_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
are
p
-linearly independent
if for all
c_{1},\dots,c_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}/p
,
c_{1}h_{1}+\dots+c_{k}h_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}

implies that
c_{1},\dots,c_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}
, or equivalently, if
\iota(h_{1}),\dots,\iota(h_{k})
are linearly independent.

2.
The relation graph for additive tuples

2.1.
A non-transitive relation for shifted
M
-modules

Before proving Theorem
1.5
, we need to rephraze the problem using some new terminology to be defined in this section. Throughout this paper,
p
is a prime,
d
is the dimension of the vector field over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
and
\mathbb{Q}
,
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
is a non-degenerate quadratic form, and
\tilde{M}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p
is the regular lifting of
M
.
For an
M
-module
I

and a polynomial
f\in\mathbb{Q}[x_{1},\dots,x_{d}]
, we call
I+f
a
shifted
M
-module
of
\mathbb{Q}[x_{1},\dots,x_{d}]
. The space of all shifted
M
-modules of
\mathbb{Q}[x_{1},\dots,x_{d}]
is denoted as
\mathcal{J}(M)
.

We may define an “additive” operation on
\mathcal{J}(M)
as follows.
For
I+f,I^{\prime}+f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}(M)
, denote
(I+f)\hat{+}(I^{\prime}+f^{\prime}):=I+I^{\prime}+f+f^{\prime}
and
(I+f)\hat{-}(I^{\prime}+f^{\prime}):=I-I^{\prime}+f-f^{\prime}
. Clearly, both
(\mathcal{J}(M),\hat{+})
and
(\mathcal{J}(M),\hat{-})
are semi-groups, but the operations
\hat{+}
and
\hat{-}
are not the inverse of each other.
6
6

6

We write the operations as
\hat{+}
and
\hat{-}
instead of
+
and
-
to emphasize that they are not invertible.

We also need to define a relation between elements in
\mathcal{J}(M)
as follows.
Denote
I+f\sim I^{\prime}+f^{\prime}
if
f-f^{\prime}\in I+I^{\prime}
.
Clearly, if
x\sim y
and
x^{\prime}\sim y^{\prime}
, then
x\hat{+}x^{\prime}\sim y\hat{+}y^{\prime}
and
x\hat{-}x^{\prime}\sim y\hat{-}y^{\prime}
.
It is easy to see that
u\sim u
, and
u\sim v\Rightarrow v\sim u
. However, the next example shows that
\sim
is not transitive and so is not an equivalence relation.

Example 2.1
.

Let
\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}=\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
M(n)=n\cdot n
.
For all
x\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, let
L_{x}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p
denote the function given by
L_{x}(n)=\frac{1}{p}\tau(x)\cdot n
. Let
e_{1},\dots,e_{d}
be the standard unit vectors of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
. Then
J^{M}_{e_{1}}+L_{e_{2}}\sim J^{M}_{e_{2}}
and
J^{M}_{e_{2}}\sim J^{M}_{e_{3}}
. However, by Lemma
A.6
and Proposition
A.8
, it is not hard to see that
J^{M}_{e_{1}}+L_{e_{2}}\not\sim J^{M}_{e_{3}}
if
d\gg 1
and
p\gg_{d}1
. So
\sim
is not transitive.

The above mentioned operations and relation can be extended naturally to the set
\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}\times\mathcal{J}(M)
.
For
(h,I+f),(h^{\prime},I^{\prime}+f^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}\times\mathcal{J}(M)
, denote
(h,I+f)\hat{+}(h^{\prime},I^{\prime}+f^{\prime}):=(h+h^{\prime},I+I^{\prime}+f+f^{\prime})
and
(h,I+f)\hat{-}(h^{\prime},I^{\prime}+f^{\prime}):=(h+h^{\prime},I-I^{\prime}+f-f^{\prime})
. We write
(h,I+f)\sim(h^{\prime},I^{\prime}+f^{\prime})
if
h=h^{\prime}
and
I+f\sim I^{\prime}+f^{\prime}
.

Convention 2.2
.

Throughout this paper, we use
\pi\colon\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}\times\mathcal{J}(M)\to\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}
to denote the projection map.

In this paper, we are particularly interested in the non-transitive relation
\sim
on a special subset
\Gamma^{s}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M)
of
\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}\times\mathcal{J}(M)
, which denotes the set of all elements of the form
(h,J^{M}_{V}+f)
for some
h\in\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}
, some
f\in\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z}/p^{\mathbb{N}},d}(s)
, and some
M
-module
J^{M}_{V}
for some subspace
V
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
with
\iota(h)\in V
. We also need to divide the set
\Gamma^{s}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M)
further according to the dimension of
V
.
For
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+},

let
\Gamma^{s}_{k}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M)
denote the set of all
(h,J^{M}_{V}+f)\in\Gamma^{s}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M),\iota(h)\neq\mathbb{0}
with
\dim(V)\leq k
union the set of all
(h,J^{M}_{V}+f)\in\Gamma^{s}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M),\iota(h)=\mathbb{0}
with
(V)\leq k-1
.
Equivalently,
\Gamma^{s}_{k}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M)
is the set of all
(h,J^{M}_{V}+f)\in\Gamma^{s}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M),h\in\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}
where
V
can be written as the span of
k
vectors
h_{1},\dots,h_{k}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
with one of them being
\iota(h)
.
In particular,
\Gamma^{s}_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M)
consists of all elements of the form
(h,J^{M}_{\iota(h)}+f),h\in\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},f\in\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z}/p^{\mathbb{N}},d}(s)
.

Example 2.3
.

Let
\xi_{i}\colon H\to\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z}/p^{\mathbb{N}},d}(s),1\leq i\leq 4
be given as in Theorem
1.5
. Let
\tilde{\xi}_{i}\colon H\to\Gamma^{s}_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M)
be the map given by
\tilde{\xi}_{i}(h):=(h,\xi_{i}(h)+J^{M}_{\iota(h)})
. Informally speaking, the assumption in Theorem
1.5
is equivalent of saying that the “additive energy” of
\tilde{\xi}_{i}(H)\hat{+}\tilde{\xi}_{i}(H)
(i.e. the number of quadraples
(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4})\in H^{4}
with
\tilde{\xi}_{i}(h_{1})\hat{+}\tilde{\xi}_{i}(h_{2})\sim\tilde{\xi}_{i}(h_{3})\hat{+}\tilde{\xi}_{i}(h_{4})
) is at least
\delta|H|^{3}
.

We summarize some basic properties for later uses.
The following lemma is straightforward:

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}
and
k,k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
.
If
a\in\Gamma^{s}_{k}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M)
and
b\in\Gamma^{s}_{k^{\prime}}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M)
, then
a\hat{+}b\in\Gamma^{s}_{k+k^{\prime}}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M)
.

Proof.

Assume that
a=(h,J^{M}_{V}+f)
and
b=(h^{\prime},J^{M}_{V^{\prime}}+f^{\prime})
for some
h,h^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}
,
f,f^{\prime}\in\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z}/p^{\mathbb{N}},d}(s)
and subspaces
V,V^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
with
\iota(h)\in V
and
\iota(h)^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}
. Then
\iota(h)+\iota(h^{\prime})\in V+V^{\prime}
.

If
\iota(h)=\mathbb{0}
, then
\dim(V)\leq k-1
. Since
\dim(V^{\prime})\leq k^{\prime}
, we have
\dim(V+V^{\prime})\leq(k+k^{\prime})-1
. Similarly,
\dim(V+V^{\prime})\leq(k+k^{\prime})-1
if
\iota(h^{\prime})=\mathbb{0}
. In both cases, we have
a\hat{+}b\in\Gamma^{s}_{k+k^{\prime}}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M)
.

We now assume that
\iota(h),\iota(h^{\prime})\neq\mathbb{0}
.
Then
\dim(V)\leq k
and
\dim(V^{\prime})\leq k^{\prime}
.
Since
\dim(V+V^{\prime})\leq k^{\prime}+k
, we have that
a\hat{+}b\in\Gamma^{s}_{k+k^{\prime}}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M)
if
\iota(h+h^{\prime})\neq\mathbb{0}
. If
\iota(h+h^{\prime})=\mathbb{0}
, then
V\cap V^{\prime}\neq\{\mathbb{0}\}
and so
\dim(V+V^{\prime})\leq k^{\prime}+k-1
. So we still have that
a\hat{+}b\in\Gamma^{s}_{k+k^{\prime}}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M)
.
∎

Although
\sim
is not a transitive relation in general, for the following special case it is:

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
x_{1},x_{2}\in\Gamma^{s}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M)
and
y\in\Gamma^{s}_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M)
.
If
x_{1}\sim y
and
x_{2}\sim y
, then
x_{1}\sim x_{2}
. In particular,
\sim
is an equivalence relation on
\Gamma^{s}_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M)
.

Proof.

Write
x_{i}=(v_{i},J^{M}_{V_{i}}+f_{i})
for
i=1,2
and let
u=\pi(y)
. If
x_{1}\sim y
and
x_{2}\sim y
, then
v_{1}=u=v_{2}
. Since
\iota(v_{i})\in V_{i}
, we have that
\iota(u)\in V_{1}\cap V_{2}
. So for
i=1,2
,
x_{i}\equiv y\mod J^{M}_{V_{i}+\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\iota(u)\}}=J^{M}_{V_{i}}
. So
x_{1}\equiv y\equiv x_{2}\mod J^{M}_{V_{1}+V_{2}}
and thus
x_{1}\sim x_{2}
.
∎

2.2.
The relation graph of a subset of
\Gamma^{s}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M)

The level sets (i.e. sets of the form
\{(a,a^{\prime})\in A\times A\colon a+a^{\prime}=b\}
for some
b\in G
) play an important role in the study of conventional additive combinatorics. In our setting, we do not have such a convenience tool. This is because the operation
\hat{+}
does not have the cancellation property (i.e.
a\hat{+}b\sim a^{\prime}\hat{+}b\not\Rightarrow a\sim a^{\prime}
), and the relation
\sim
is not an equivalent relation.
To overcome this difficulty, we use a graph to characterize the relation
\sim
.

Definition 2.6

(Relation graph)
.

Let
X
be a subset of
\Gamma^{s}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M)
. we say that a graph
G=(X,E)
is the
relation graph
of
X
if for all
u,v\in X,u\neq v
,
\{u,v\}\in E
if and only if
u\sim v
.

Remark 2.7
.

The structure of relation graphs for the conventional additive combinatorics on abelian groups is quite simple. In fact,
if
\sim
is an equivalence relation on
X
, then the graph
G
is a disjoint union of complete subgraphs, each of which represent an equivalence class (or a level set). However, in our setting, the behavior of the relation graph can be very complicated.

Since we need to frequently partition a relation graph into smaller components, it is helpful to introduce the following notion.

Definition 2.8

(Graph partitions)
.

Let
G=(X,E)
be a graph and
G_{i}=(X_{i},E_{i}),1\leq i\leq k
be subgraphs of
G
. We say that
G_{1},\dots,G_{k}
is a
disjoint partition
of
G
if
X_{1},\dots,X_{k}
are pairwise disjoint,
X=\cup_{i=1}^{k}X_{i}
and
E=\cup_{i=1}^{k}E_{i}
.

Similarly, Let
G=(X,Y,E)
be a bipartite graph and
G_{i}=(X_{i},Y_{i},E_{i}),1\leq i\leq k
be subgraphs of
G
. We say that
G_{1},\dots,G_{k}
is a
disjoint partition
of
G
if
X_{1},\dots,X_{k},Y_{1},\dots,Y_{k}
are pairwise disjoint,
X=\cup_{i=1}^{k}X_{i}
,
Y=\cup_{i=1}^{k}Y_{i}
and
E=\cup_{i=1}^{k}E_{i}
.

Convention 2.9
.

Let
G=(X,E)
be a graph and
X_{1},\dots,X_{k}
be pairwise disjoint and
X=\cup_{i=1}^{k}X_{i}
. When we say that
G_{i}=(X_{i},E_{i}),1\leq i\leq k
is a disjoint partition of
G
, we implicitly indicate that
E_{i}
is the set of all edges in
E
with both vertices coming from
X_{i}
.

Similarly, Let
G=(X,Y,E)
be a bipartite graph and
X_{1},\dots,X_{k},Y_{1},\dots,Y_{k}
be pairwise disjoint,
X=\cup_{i=1}^{k}X_{i}
and
Y=\cup_{i=1}^{k}Y_{i}
. When we say that
G_{i}=(X_{i},Y_{i},E_{i}),1\leq i\leq k
is a disjoint partition of
G
, we implicitly indicate that
E_{i}
is the set of all edges in
E
with one vertex from
X_{i}
and the other from
Y_{i}
.

Remark 2.10
.

Let
X
be a subset of
\Gamma^{s}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M)
.
Note that for
u,v\in X
, we have that
u\sim v
only if
\pi(u)=\pi(v)
. Therefore, the relation graph
G=(X,E)
has a disjoint partition induced by the partition
\cup_{h\in\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}}\pi^{-1}(h)\cap X
of
X
. So it is helpful to think of
G
as the union of
K^{d}
disjoint smaller graphs.

Next we introduce two quantities for graphs which play important roles in this paper.
Recall that in graph theory, the
(vertex) independence number
of a graph is the maximum number of vertices which are pairwise disjoint.
The first quantity we need is a variation of the concept of independence number:

Definition 2.11

(Auxiliary graph and density dependence number)
.

Let
G=(X,E)
be a graph and
G^{\prime}=(X,Y,E^{\prime})
be a bipartite graph, we say that
G^{\prime}
is an
auxiliary graph
of
G
if for all
x,x^{\prime}\in X
and
y\in Y
, if
(x,y),(x^{\prime},y)\in E^{\prime}
, then
\{x,x^{\prime}\}\in E
.
For
\epsilon>0
, we say that
G^{\prime}
is
\epsilon
-dense
if for every
x\in X
, there exist at least
\epsilon|Y|
many
y\in Y
such that
(x,y)\in E^{\prime}
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
h\in\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}
and
G=(X,E)
be the relation graph of a subset
X
of
\Gamma^{s}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M)
such that
\pi(X)=\{h\}
.
The
density dependence number
of
G
(or
X
), denoted by
{\#}_{\text{dd}}(G)
(or
{\#}_{\text{dd}}(X)
), is the smallest integer
C>0
such that
G
admits a
C^{-1}
-dense auxiliary graph.

For the relation graph
G=(X,E)
of a general subset
X
of
\Gamma^{s}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M)
. We define
{\#}_{\text{dd}}(G)
(or
{\#}_{\text{dd}}(X)
) as the maximum of
{\#}_{\text{dd}}(\pi^{-1}(h)\cap X),h\in\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}
.

By the Pigeonhole Principle, it is not hard to see that the independence number of a graph
G
is at most
{\#}_{\text{dd}}(G)
. It is natural to ask about the converse direction:

Question 2.12
.

For any graph, is the independent number equal to
{\#}_{\text{dd}}(G)
? If not, then is the independent number bounded below by a constant depending on
{\#}_{\text{dd}}(G)
?

The second quantity we need is one that describes the optimal way in which a graph can be covered by cliques.
Recall that in graph theory,
a
clique
of a graph
G=(X,E)
is a subset
S
of
X
such that the subgraph of
G
induced by
S
is a complete graph. In this paper, we measure the largeness of graph by looking at the minimal number of clique we need to cover all the vertices.

Definition 2.13

(Clique cover number)
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
h\in\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}
and
G=(X,E)
be the relation graph of a subset
X
of
\Gamma^{s}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M)
such that
\pi(X)=\{h\}
. The
clique cover number
of
G
(or
X
), denoted by
{\#}_{\text{cc}}(G)
(or
{\#}_{\text{cc}}(X)
) is the smallest integer
C
such that we may partition
X
into
\sqcup_{i=0}^{C}X_{i}
such that each
X_{i}
is a clique of
G
.

For the relation graph
G=(X,E)
of a general subset
X
of
\Gamma^{s}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M)
. We define
{\#}_{\text{cc}}(G)
(or
{\#}_{\text{cc}}(X)
) as the maximum of
{\#}_{\text{cc}}(\pi^{-1}(h)\cap X),h\in\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}
.

We summarize some properties on
the clique cover number and density dependence number of a graph for later uses. The following lemma is straightforward.

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}
and
G=(X,E)
be the relation graph of a non-empty subset
X
of
\Gamma^{s}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M)
. Then

(1)

{\#}_{\text{dd}}(G)
is finite and
{\#}_{\text{cc}}(G)\geq{\#}_{\text{dd}}(G)\geq 1
;

(2)

for any subgraph
G^{\prime}
of
G
, we have that
{\#}_{\text{cc}}(G^{\prime})\leq{\#}_{\text{cc}}(G)
and
{\#}_{\text{dd}}(G^{\prime})\leq{\#}_{\text{dd}}(G)
;

(3)

if
\pi(X)=\{h\}
for some
h\in\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}
, then any subset
X^{\prime}
of
X
contains a clique of size at least
\lceil\frac{|X^{\prime}|}{{\#}_{\text{dd}}(G)}\rceil
;

(4)

{\#}_{\text{dd}}(G)=1\Leftrightarrow{\#}_{\text{cc}}(G)=1\Leftrightarrow u\sim v
for all
h\in\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}
and
u,v\in\pi^{-1}(h)\cap X
.

Proof.

We first prove Part (i).
Assume without loss of generality that
\pi(X)=\{h\}
for some
h\in\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}
.
Clearly,

{\#}_{\text{dd}}(G)\geq 1
. Note that we may partition
X
into
\sqcup_{i=1}^{{\#}_{\text{cc}}(G)}X_{i}
such that each
X_{i}
is a clique of
G
.
Let
G^{\prime}=(X,\{1,\dots,{\#}_{\text{cc}}(G)\},E^{\prime})
be the bipartite graph given by
(x,i)\in E^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow x\in X_{i}
. Since each
X_{i}
is a clique of
G
, we have that
G^{\prime}
is an auxiliary graph of
G
. On the other hand, the density of this auxiliary graph is
\frac{1}{{\#}_{\text{cc}}(G)}
. So we have that
{\#}_{\text{dd}}(G)
is finite and
{\#}_{\text{cc}}(G)\geq{\#}_{\text{dd}}(G)
.

Part (ii) is straightforward. For Part (iii), let
G^{\prime}=(X,Y,E^{\prime})
be an auxiliary graph of
G
of density at least
{\#}_{\text{dd}}(G)^{-1}
. Then there are at least
\frac{|X^{\prime}|\cdot|Y|}{{\#}_{\text{dd}}(G)}
edges in
E^{\prime}
with one of the vertices coming from
X^{\prime}
. By the Pigeonhole Principle, there exists
y\in Y
which is connected by edges in
E^{\prime}
to at least
\lceil\frac{|X^{\prime}|}{{\#}_{\text{dd}}(G)}\rceil
vertices in
X^{\prime}
. These vertices induces a clique in
G
by the definition of auxiliary graphs.

Part (iv) follows from the definitions and Part (iii).
∎

The next lemma allows us to expand an auxiliary graph without changing its density.

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}
and
X
be a subset of
\Gamma^{s}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M)
with
\pi(X)=\{h\}
for some
h\in\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}
,
\epsilon>0
, and
G^{\prime}=(X,Y,E^{\prime})
be an
\epsilon
-dense auxiliary graph of
X
. Then for all
N\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
divisible by
|Y|
,
X
admits an
\epsilon
-dense auxiliary graph
G^{\prime\prime}=(X,Y^{\prime},E^{\prime\prime})
with
|Y^{\prime}|=N
.

Proof.

Denote
m:=N/|Y|
and let
Y^{\prime}=\sqcup_{i=1}^{m}Y_{i}
be
m
identical copies of
Y
and let
\iota_{i}\colon Y_{i}\to Y
be the bijection for
1\leq i\leq m
. For
x\in X
and
y\in Y^{\prime}
, set
(x,y)\in E^{\prime\prime}
if and only if
y
belongs to some
Y_{i}
and
(x,\iota_{i}(y))\in E^{\prime}
. It is not hard to see that
|Y^{\prime}|=N
and
G^{\prime\prime}=(X,Y^{\prime},E^{\prime\prime})
is an
\epsilon
-dense auxiliary graph of
X
.
∎

Let
X
be a subset of
\Gamma^{s}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M)
.
If we add an extra point
x
to
X
such that
x\not\sim y
for all
y\in X
, then it is clear that the clique cover number of
X
is increased by 1. In this case, it turns out that the density dependence number of
X
is also increased by 1.

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}
and
X
be a subset of
\Gamma^{s}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M)
with
\pi(X)=\{h\}
for some
h\in\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}
. Let
y\in\Gamma^{s}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M)
with
\pi(y)=h
be such that
x\not\sim y
for all
x\in X
. Then
{\#}_{\text{dd}}(X\cup\{y\})={\#}_{\text{dd}}(X)+1
.

Proof.

For convenience denote
C:={\#}_{\text{dd}}(X)
and
D:={\#}_{\text{dd}}(X\cup\{y\})
. Then

X\cup\{y\}
admits a
D^{-1}
-dense auxiliary graph

G^{\prime}=(X\cup\{y\},Z,E^{\prime})
. Let
Z^{\prime}:=\{z\in Z\colon(y,z)\in E^{\prime}\}
and
Z^{\prime\prime}:=Z\backslash Z^{\prime}
. Then
|Z^{\prime}|\geq D^{-1}|Z|
. Since
y\not\sim x
for all
x\in X
, we have that
(x,z)\notin E^{\prime}
for all
x\in X
and
z\in Z^{\prime}
.
Consider the bipartite graph
G^{\prime\prime}=(X,Z^{\prime\prime},E^{\prime\prime})
induced by
G^{\prime}
.
Since
(x,z)\notin E^{\prime}
for all
x\in X
and
z\in Z^{\prime}
, it is not hard to see that
G^{\prime\prime}
is an auxiliary graph of
X
, and that the density of this auxiliary graph is at least

\frac{D^{-1}|Z|}{|Z|-|Z^{\prime}|}\geq\frac{D^{-1}|Z|}{|Z|-D^{-1}|Z|}=(D-1)^{-1}.

This implies that
C\leq D-1
.

Conversely, since
{\#}_{\text{dd}}(X)=C
,
X
admits a
C^{-1}
-dense auxiliary graph
G^{\prime}=(X,Z,E^{\prime})
. By Lemma
2.15
, we may assume without loss of generality that
|Z|
is divisible by
C
. Let
Z^{\prime}
be any set of cardinality
|Z|/C
which is disjoint from
Z
, and let
G^{\prime\prime}=(X\cup\{y\},Z\sqcup Z^{\prime},E^{\prime\prime})
be the bipartite graph such that for all
x\in X\cup\{y\}
and
z\in Z\sqcup Z^{\prime}
,
(x,z)\in E^{\prime\prime}
if and only if either (i)
x\in X
,
z\in Z
and
(x,z)\in E^{\prime}
; or (ii)
x=y
and
z\in Z^{\prime}
. Clearly,
G^{\prime\prime}
is an auxiliary graph of
X\cup\{y\}
. On the other hand, the density of
G^{\prime\prime}
is

\frac{\min\{C^{-1}|Z|,|Z^{\prime}|\}}{|Z|+|Z^{\prime}|}=\frac{C^{-1}|Z|}{|Z|+C^{-1}|Z|}=(C+1)^{-1}.

This implies that
D\leq C+1
.

Combining both directions, we have that
D=C+1
.
∎

2.3.
Outline of the proof of Theorem
1.5

We are now ready to provide an overview for the proof of Theorem
1.5
:

Step 0.

Our first goal is to solve the
Freiman equation
(see (
4.4
)) on a generalized arithmetic progression (see Theorem
4.7
).
This can be viewed as a special case of Theorem
1.5
when
U
equals to
\{(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4})\in H^{4}\colon h_{1}+h_{2}=h_{3}+h_{4}\}
,
H
is a generalized arithmetic progression, and
\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}=\xi_{3}=\xi_{4}
.
This result will be used later in Step 4 to be described below.
While the the Freiman equation for abelian groups (see (
4.2
)) can be solved easily, the Freiman equation is the setting of shifted modules is much harder. This step is done in Section
4
.

Step 1.
We show that if the conditions of Theorem
1.5
are satisfied, then there exists a large subset
H^{\prime}
of
H
such that
\pi^{-1}(\mathbb{0})\cap(8\tilde{\xi}(H^{\prime})\hat{-}8\tilde{\xi}(H^{\prime}))
has a small density dependence number, where
\tilde{\xi}(h):=(h,J^{M}_{\iota(h)}+\xi_{1}(h))\in\Gamma^{s}_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d},M)
. To achieve this, we need to extend many classical results in combinatorics to shifted modules, including the Balog-Gowers-Szemerédi Theorem, the Ruzsa triangle inequality, the Green-Ruzsa inequality, and the Plünnecke-Rusza theorem. This is done in Section
5
.

Step 2.
We show that if
\pi^{-1}(\mathbb{0})\cap(8\tilde{\xi}(H^{\prime})\hat{-}8\tilde{\xi}(H^{\prime}))
has a small density dependence number, then we may decompose the set
\pi^{-1}(\mathbb{0})\cap(8\tilde{\xi}(H^{\prime})\hat{-}8\tilde{\xi}(H^{\prime}))
into two parts. The first is the structure part with a small clique covering number, and the second is the obstacle part which has nontrivial intersections with an object with low dimension. This is done in Section
6
. Unfortunately we were unable to remove the obstacle part, and thus we need carry this error term throughout the proof, which makes the proof of the remaining steps more technical. This step is done in Section
6
.

Step 3.
We show that if
\pi^{-1}(\mathbb{0})\cap(8\tilde{\xi}(H^{\prime})\hat{-}8\tilde{\xi}(H^{\prime}))
has a decomposition described in Step 2, then for many “good” subspaces
V
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, there exists a large subset
H^{\prime\prime}
of
H^{\prime}
such that
\xi
is a Freiman
M
-homomorphism of order 16 on
H^{\prime\prime}\cap\iota^{-1}(V)
. On the other hand, we show that
8H^{\prime\prime}-8H^{\prime\prime}
has rich generalized arithmetic progression structures. The proof is inspired by the constructions of appropriate Bohr sets introduced in
[
3
]
. This step is done in Section
7
.

Step 4.
If we could show that
\xi
is a Freiman
M
-homomorphism of order 16 on a large subset
H^{\prime\prime}
of
H^{\prime}
(instead of on
H^{\prime\prime}\cap\iota^{-1}(V)
), then one can directly apply the solutions to Freiman equations obtained in Step 0 to conclude that
\xi
is locally linear, and thus Theorem
1.5
can be proved easily. Unfortunately the subspaces
V
in Step 3 can not be taken to be
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
. To overcome this difficulty, we first solve for
\xi
on “good” subspaces
V
, and then combine this information on different
V
to conclude that
\xi
is a Freiman
M
-homomorphism of order 16 on a large subset
H^{\prime\prime}
of
H^{\prime}
, modulo a module of the form
J^{M}_{T}
for some subspace
T
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.
This is done is Section
8
.

Step 5.
By intersecting the Freiman
M
-homomorphism structure of
\xi
obtained in Step 4 for many different
J^{M}_{T}
, we conclude that
\xi
is a genial Freiman
M
-homomorphism of order 16. We may then invoke Theorem
4.7
to show that
\xi
is locally linear and thus complete the proof of Theorem
1.5
.
This is done is Section
9
.

3.
Intersection property for
M
-modules

We begin with the notation of linear independence for subspaces of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
:

Definition 3.1

(Linearly independent subspaces)
.

Let
V_{1},\dots,V_{k}
be subspaces of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.
We say that
V_{1},\dots,V_{k}
are
linearly independent
if for all
v_{i}\in V_{i},1\leq i\leq k
,
\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}=\mathbb{0}
implies that
v_{1}=\dots=v_{k}=\mathbb{0}
.
In this case we also say that
(V_{1},\dots,V_{k})
is a
linearly independent tuple
(or a
linearly independent pair
if
k=2
).

Let
V_{1},\dots,V_{k}
be subspaces of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
v_{1},\dots,v_{k^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.
We say that
v_{1},\dots,v_{k^{\prime}}
,
V_{1},\dots,V_{k}
are
linearly independent
if for all
c_{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{p},1\leq i\leq k
and
v^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}\in V_{i^{\prime}},1\leq i^{\prime}\leq k^{\prime}
,
\sum_{i=1}^{k}c_{i}v_{i}+\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{k^{\prime}}v^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}=\mathbb{0}
implies that
c_{1}=\dots=c_{k}=0
and
v^{\prime}_{1}=\dots=v^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}=\mathbb{0}
.
In this case we also say that

(v_{1},\dots,v_{k^{\prime}},V_{1},\dots,V_{k})
is a
linearly independent tuple
(or a
linearly independent pair
if
k+k^{\prime}=2
).
7
7

7

We caution the readers that according to our definition, the zero vector
\mathbb{0}
is linearly dependent with every subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, while the trivial subspace
\{\mathbb{0}\}
is linearly independent with every subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
. This unconventional definition turns out to be convenient for this paper.

Let
V,V_{1},\dots,V_{k}
be subspaces of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
with
V\subseteq\cap_{i=1}^{k}V_{i}
. We say that
V_{1},\dots,V_{k}
are
linearly independent modulo
V
if for all
v_{i}\in V_{i},1\leq i\leq k
,
\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{k}=\mathbb{0}
implies that
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\in V
.

In order to illustrate the main idea of this section, it is helpful to
consider the following motivative question:

Question 3.2
.

Let
V
be a subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
V_{1},\dots,V_{N}
be nontrivial linearly independent subspaces of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
. When do we have that
\cap_{i=1}^{N}(V+V_{i})=V
?

For example, let
N=2
and
V,V_{1}
and
V_{2}
be the
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-span of
(1,0,\dots,0)
,
(0,1,0,\dots,0)
and
(1,1,0,\dots,0)
respectively. It is clear that
(V+V_{1})\cap(V+V_{2})=\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{e_{1},e_{2}\}\neq V=\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{e_{1}\}
. However, if we add an addition subspace
V_{3}=\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{h\}
with
V_{1},V_{2},V_{3}
being linearly independent, then this will force
h
not to belong to
\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
, which implies that
\cap_{i=1}^{3}(V+V_{i})=V
. Motivated by this example, it is natural to expect that
\cap_{i=1}^{N}(V+V_{i})=V
whenever
N
is sufficiently large. Indeed, we have

Proposition 3.3

(Intersection property for subspaces of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
)
.

Let
V
be a subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
V_{1},\dots,V_{N}
be linearly independent subspaces of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
. If
N\geq\dim(V)+2
, then
\cap_{i=1}^{N}(V+V_{i})=V
.

Informally, one can think of the unions of
V+V_{i}
as a “sunflower” with
V
being its “core” and
V_{i}
being its “petals”. The philosophy behind Proposition
3.3
is that if we take the intersection of sufficiently many linearly independent “petals”, then eventually the intersection will decrease to
V
. Therefore, we call Proposition
3.3
the
intersection property
for subspaces.

To prove Proposition
3.3
, it suffices to show the following lemma (and then set
s=2
):

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
m,s\in\mathbb{N}
,
d,N\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
p
be a prime, and
V,V_{1},\dots,V_{N}
be subspaces of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
such that
\dim(V)=m
. Suppose that
V_{1},\dots,V_{N}
are linearly independent and
N\geq m+s
, then there exist
\{i_{1},\dots,i_{s}\}\subseteq\{1,\dots,N\}
such that
V,V_{i_{1}},\dots,V_{i_{s}}

are linearly independent.

Proof.

There is nothing to prove when
s=0
.
Suppose that the conclusion holds for some
s\in\mathbb{N}
when
N\geq m+s
. We prove that the conclusion holds for
s+1
when
N\geq m+s+1
.

By induction hypothesis, if
N\geq m+s
, then we may assume without loss of generality that
V,V_{1},\dots,V_{s}
are linearly independent. Suppose that for all
1\leq i\leq N
,
V,V_{1},\dots,V_{s},V_{i}
are not linearly independent. Then there exists a nontrivial
v_{i}\in V_{i}\cap(V+V_{1}+\dots+V_{s})
for all
s+1\leq i\leq N
. Since
\dim(V)=m
, if
N-s\geq m+1
, then
there exist
c_{s+1},\dots,c_{N}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
not all equal to 0 such that

\sum_{i=s+1}^{N}c_{i}v_{i}\in V_{1}+\dots+V_{s}
. Since
V_{1},\dots,V_{N}
are linearly independent, we have that
c_{i}=0
for all
s+1\leq i\leq N
, a contradiction. Therefore, if
N\geq m+s+1
, then there must be some
1\leq j\leq N
such that
V,V_{1},\dots,V_{s},V_{j}
are linearly independent.
∎

Remark 3.5
.

We remark that the lower bound
N\geq m+s
in Lemma
3.4
is optimal. To see this, let
V=\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{e_{1},\dots,e_{m}\}
and
V_{i}=\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{e_{i}\}
for
1\leq i\leq m+s-1
, where
e_{j}
is the
j
-th standard unit vector. Then
V_{1},\dots,V_{m+s-1}
are linearly independent, but for any
s
of
V_{1},\dots,V_{m+s-1}
, since at least one of them comes from
V_{1},\dots,V_{m}
, it has nontrivial intersection with
V
.

Following the philosophy of Proposition
3.3
, it is natural to ask for the intersection property for shifted modules.
We have the following, which is the main result of this section.

Proposition 3.6

(Intersection property for weakly independent
M
-modules)
.

Let
m,s\in\mathbb{N}
,
d,N,r\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
p\gg_{d}1
be a prime,
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a non-degenerate quadratic form, and
V,V_{1},\dots,V_{N}
be subspaces of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
with
V_{1},\dots,V_{N}
being linearly independent such that
\dim(V)=m
and
\dim(V_{i})\leq r,1\leq i\leq N
. Suppose that
N\geq s+m+1
and that
either
\text{rank}(M|_{V^{\perp_{M}}})\geq 2N(r-1)+7
or
d\geq 2m+2N(r-1)+7
.
8
8

8

See Appendix
A.1
for definitions.
Then for all
f\in\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z}/p^{\mathbb{N}},d}(s)
, we have that

f\in\cap_{i=1}^{N}J^{M}_{V+V_{i}}\Leftrightarrow f\in J^{M}_{V}.

It is an interesting question to ask what is the best lower bound of
d
for Proposition
3.6
. although we do not pursuit this in the paper.

By Lemma
3.4
, to prove Proposition
3.6
, it suffices to show follows the following weaker version of it:

Proposition 3.7

(Intersection property for strongly independent
M
-modules)
.

Let
m,s\in\mathbb{N}
,
d,N,r\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
p\gg_{d}1
be a prime,
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a non-degenerate quadratic form, and
V,V_{1},\dots,V_{N}
be linearly independent subspaces of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
such that
\dim(V)=m
and
\dim(V_{i})\leq r,1\leq i\leq N
. Suppose that
N\geq s+1
and that
either
\text{rank}(M|_{V^{\perp_{M}}})\geq 2N(r-1)+7
or
d\geq 2m+2N(r-1)+7
.
Then for all
f\in\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z}/p^{\mathbb{N}},d}(s)
, we have that

f\in\cap_{i=1}^{N}J^{M}_{V+V_{i}}\Leftrightarrow f\in J^{M}_{V}.

We need some preparation before proving Proposition
3.7
. The next lemma says that if
h,h_{1},\dots,h_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}
are
p
-linearly independent, then the degree 1 polynomial
L_{h}(n):=\frac{(n\tau(A))\cdot h}{p}
is “irreducible” in
J^{M}_{\iota(h_{1}),\dots,\iota(h_{k})}
.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
k,s\in\mathbb{N}
,
p\gg_{d,k,s}1
be a prime,
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a non-degenerate quadratic form associated with the matrix
A
, and
h,h_{1},\dots,h_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
be
p
-linearly independent vectors.
Let
f\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Q}
be a
\mathbb{Z}/p^{\mathbb{N}}
-coefficient polynomial of degree at most
s

such that
pL_{h}f\in J^{M}_{\iota(h_{1}),\dots,\iota(h_{k})}
, where
L_{h}(n):=\frac{(n\tau(A))\cdot h}{p}
. If

\text{rank}(M|_{\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\iota(h),\iota(h_{1}),\dots,\iota(h_{k})\}^{\perp_{M}}})\geq 3
or
d\geq 2k+5
, then we have that
f\in J^{M}_{\iota(h_{1}),\dots,\iota(h_{k})}
.

Proof.

We use the
p
-expansion trick introduced in
[
7
]
.
With may assume without loss of generality that
f
is homogeneous, and that
\deg(f)=s
.
Let
\tilde{M}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p
be the quadratic form given by
\tilde{M}(n):=\frac{1}{p}((nA)\cdot n)
.
Denote
L_{h_{j}}(n):=\frac{(n\tau(A))\cdot h}{p}
for all
1\leq j\leq k
.
We say that
g
is
good
if

g=\sum_{i:=(i_{0},\dots,i_{k})\in\mathbb{N}^{k+1},2i_{0}+i_{1}+\dots+i_{k}\leq s}R_{i}\tilde{M}^{i_{0}}L_{h_{1}}^{i_{1}}\dots L_{h_{k}}^{i_{k}}

for some integer coefficient polynomials
R_{i}
with
\deg(R_{i})=s-(2i_{0}+i_{1}+\dots+i_{k})
.
Let
t
be the smallest positive integer such that
f
can be written as

f=\sum_{i=0}^{t}\frac{f_{i}}{p^{i}}

for some good polynomial
f_{i}
of degree
s
. Clearly such
t
exists since all the coefficients of
f
are in
\mathbb{Z}/p^{\mathbb{N}}
. Our goal is to show
t=0
.

Suppose on the countrary that
t>0
.
Since
f_{t}
is good, we may write

f_{t}=\sum_{i:=(i_{0},\dots,i_{k})\in\mathbb{N}^{k+1},2i_{0}+i_{1}+\dots+i_{k}\leq s}R_{i}\tilde{M}^{i_{0}}L_{h_{1}}^{i_{1}}\dots L_{h_{k}}^{i_{k}}

for some integer coefficient polynomials
R_{i}
with
\deg(R_{i})=s-(2i_{0}+i_{1}+\dots+i_{k})
.
Since
p^{t}L_{h}f\equiv L_{h}f_{t}\mod\mathbb{Z}
, we have that
L_{h}(n)f_{t}(n)\in\mathbb{Z}
whenever
M(n),L_{h_{i}}(n)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
1\leq i\leq k
.
Let
V
be the span of
\iota(h_{1}),\dots,\iota(h_{k})
. Then for any
n\in V_{p}(\tilde{M})\cap\iota^{-1}(V^{\perp_{M}})
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we have that
pL_{h}(n+pm)f_{t}(n+pm)\in\mathbb{Z}
. Note that
pL_{h}(n+pm)\equiv(n\tau(A))\cdot h\mod\mathbb{Z}
and

(3.1)

\begin{split}&\quad\frac{1}{p}f_{t}(n+pm)\equiv\\
&\frac{1}{p}\sum_{i:=(i_{0},\dots,i_{k})\in\mathbb{N}^{k+1},2i_{0}+i_{1}+\dots+i_{k}\leq s}R_{i}(n)(M(n)+2(n\tau(A))\cdot m)^{i_{0}}\prod_{j=1}^{k}(L_{h_{j}}(n)+(h_{j}\tau(A))\cdot m)^{i_{j}}\mod\mathbb{Z}\end{split}

if
n\in V_{p}(\tilde{M})\cap\iota^{-1}(V^{\perp_{M}})
.
If we assume in addition that
(n\tau(A))\cdot h\notin p\mathbb{Z}
and that
n,h_{1},\dots,h_{k}
are
p
-linearly independent, then the map
m\mapsto((n\tau(A))\cdot m,(h_{1}\tau(A))\cdot m,\dots,(h_{k}\tau(A))\cdot m)\mod p\mathbb{Z}^{k+1}
is a surjection from
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
to
[p]^{k+1}
. So if we further assume that
(n\tau(A))\cdot h\notin p\mathbb{Z}
, then it follows from (
3.1
) and the assumption that that

(3.2)

\begin{split}\frac{1}{p}\sum_{i:=(i_{0},\dots,i_{k})\in\mathbb{N}^{k+1},2i_{0}+i_{1}+\dots+i_{k}\leq s}R_{i}(n)(M(n)+x_{0})^{i_{0}}\prod_{j=1}^{k}(L_{h_{j}}(n)+x_{j})^{i_{j}}\in\mathbb{Z}\end{split}

for all
x_{0},\dots,x_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. Since (
3.2
) is a polynomial in
x_{0},\dots,x_{k}
with
\mathbb{Z}/p
-coefficients (where
n
is fixed), by Lemma
A.4
, all the coefficients of this polynomial must be in
\mathbb{Z}
. Therefore, we must have that
R_{i}(n),R(n)\in p\mathbb{Z}
. So in conclusion, we have that
R_{i}(n),R(n)\in p\mathbb{Z}
for all
n\in V_{p}(M)\cap\iota^{-1}(V^{\perp_{M}})
with
(n\tau(A))\cdot h\notin p\mathbb{Z}
and with
n,h_{1},\dots,h_{k}
being
p
-linearly independent.

We claim that
R_{i}(n),R(n)\in p\mathbb{Z}
for all
n\in V_{p}(\tilde{M})\cap\iota^{-1}(V^{\perp_{M}})
.
Let
V^{\prime}
be the set of
n\in V^{\perp_{M}}
with
(nA)\cdot\iota(h)=0
.
Since

\text{rank}(M^{\prime}|_{\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\iota(h),\iota(h_{1}),\dots,\iota(h_{k})\}^{\perp_{M}}})\geq 3
by Lemma
A.1
,
it follows from Lemma
A.5
that
|V^{\perp_{M}}|\gg_{d,k,s}p^{d-k-1}
and
|V^{\prime}|=O_{d,k,s}(p^{d-k-2})
.
Let
V^{\prime\prime}
be the set of
n\in V^{\perp_{M}}
with
n,\iota(h_{1}),\dots,\iota(h_{k})
being linearly dependent. If we can show that
|V^{\prime\prime}|=O_{d,k,s}(p^{d-k-2})
, then the claim follows from Proposition
A.8
(translated into the
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-setting).

We now estimate
|V^{\prime\prime}|
. Let
U
be the space of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
spanned by
\iota(h_{1}),\dots,\iota(h_{k})
. Then for all
n\in V^{\perp_{M}}
, we have that
n\in U^{\perp_{M}}
. If
n,\iota(h_{1}),\dots,\iota(h_{k})
are linearly dependent, then
n\in U
. So by Lemma
A.1
,
|V^{\prime\prime}|\leq|U\cap U^{\perp_{M}}|\leq p^{d-k-\text{rank}(M|_{U^{\perp_{M}}})}\leq p^{d-k-3}
. This completes the proof of the claim.

By the claim, we have that
\frac{(s!)^{d}}{p}f_{t}
is a good polynomial. Since
f_{t}
is a good polynomial, we have that all the coefficients of
\frac{1}{p}f_{t}
belong to
\frac{1}{(s!)^{d}}\mathbb{Z}\cap\frac{1}{p^{s+1}}\mathbb{Z}=\mathbb{Z}
. Therefore
\frac{1}{p}f_{t}
is also
good polynomial. We may then absorb the term
f_{t}/p^{t}
by
f_{t-1}/p^{t-1}
and get a contradiction to the minimality of
t
.

Therefore we have that
t=0
and so
f
itself is a good polynomial, which implies that
f\in J^{M}_{\iota(h_{1}),\dots,\iota(h_{k})}
.
∎

We need some definitions before proving Proposition
3.7
.

Let
A
be the matrix associated with
M
and

\tilde{M}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p
be the quadratic form given by
\tilde{M}(n):=\frac{1}{p}((n\tau(A))\cdot n)
.
For
h\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, let
L_{h}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p
be the linear map given by
L_{h}(n):=\frac{1}{p}((\tau(h)\tau(A))\cdot n)

Let
h_{1},\dots,h_{m}
be a basis of
V
, and for each
1\leq i\leq N
, let
h_{i,1},\dots,h_{i,r_{i}}
be a basis of
V_{i}
.
For
1\leq N^{\prime}\leq N
, let

\mathcal{A}_{N^{\prime}}:=\Bigl\{\tilde{M},L_{h_{1}},\dots,L_{h_{m}},p^{N^{\prime}-1}\prod_{i=1}^{N^{\prime}}L_{h_{i,j_{i}}}\colon 1\leq j_{i}\leq r_{i}\Bigr\}

and enumerate
\mathcal{A}_{N^{\prime}}
as
\{F_{N^{\prime},1},\dots,F_{N^{\prime},m_{N^{\prime}}}\}
. For
z\in\mathbb{N}
,
let
\mathcal{B}_{N^{\prime},z}
be the set of polynomials of the form

\sum_{i=(i_{1},\dots,i_{m_{N^{\prime}}})\in\mathbb{N}^{m_{N^{\prime}}}\colon w_{N^{\prime}}(i)\leq s,|i|\leq z}R_{i}\prod_{t=1}^{m_{N^{\prime}}}F_{N^{\prime},t}^{i_{t}}

for some
R_{i}\in\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z},d}(s-w_{N^{\prime}}(i))
, where
w_{N^{\prime}}(i):=\sum_{t=1}^{m_{N^{\prime}}}\deg(F_{N^{\prime},t})i_{t}
.

The key to the proof of Proposition
3.7
is the following Hilbert Nullstellensatz type result for the intersection of
M
-deals.

Proposition 3.9
.

Let the notations be the same as in Proposition
3.7
and as above.
Then for all
1\leq N^{\prime}\leq N
and
f\in\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z}/p^{z},d}(s)
with
f\in\cap_{i=1}^{N^{\prime}}J_{V+V_{i}}^{M}
, we have that
f\in\mathcal{B}_{N^{\prime},J}
.
In particular, we may write
f=f_{1}+f_{2}
for some
f_{1}\in\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z}/p^{z},d}(s)\cap J^{M}_{V}
and
f_{2}\in\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z}/p^{z},d}(s)\cap\cap_{i=1}^{N^{\prime}}J^{M}_{V_{i}}
.

Proof.

Throughout the proof we assume that
p\gg_{d,s}1
.
By Lemma
A.1
(iii), it suffices to consider the case when
\text{rank}(M|_{V^{\perp_{M}}})\geq 2N(r-1)+7
.
By Lemma
A.2
,
\text{rank}(M|_{(V+V_{1})^{\perp_{M}}})\geq 3
.

Since

\mathcal{A}_{1}:=\Bigl\{\tilde{M},L_{h_{1}},\dots,L_{h_{m}},L_{h_{1,1}},\dots,L_{h_{1,r_{1}}}\Bigr\},

by Proposition
A.9
, Proposition
3.9
holds for
N^{\prime}=1
. Suppose we have shown that the conclusion holds for some
1\leq N^{\prime}\leq N-1
. We now show that the conclusion holds for
N^{\prime}+1
.
Pick any
f\in\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z}/p^{z},d}(s)\cap\cap_{i=1}^{N^{\prime}+1}J^{M}_{V+V_{i}}
. Since the conclusion holds for
N^{\prime}
,
we may write

(3.3)

f=\sum_{i=(i_{1},\dots,i_{m_{N^{\prime}}})\in\mathbb{N}^{m_{N^{\prime}}}\colon w_{N^{\prime}}(i)\leq s,|i|\leq z}f_{i}\prod_{t=1}^{m_{N^{\prime}}}F_{N^{\prime},t}^{i_{t}}

for some
f_{i}\in\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z},d}(s-w_{N^{\prime}}(i))
.
By Lemma
A.2
,
\text{rank}(M|_{(V+V_{N+1})^{\perp_{M}}})\geq 3
.
Since
f\in J^{M}_{V+V_{N+1}}
, by Proposition
A.9
, we may also write

(3.4)

f=\sum_{j=(j_{0},\dots,j_{r_{N^{\prime}+1}})\in\mathbb{N}^{r_{N^{\prime}+1}+1}\colon 2j_{0}+j_{1}+\dots+j_{r_{N^{\prime}+1}}\leq s,|j|\leq z}g_{j}\tilde{M}^{j_{0}}L_{h_{N^{\prime}+1,1}}^{j_{1}}\dots L_{h_{N^{\prime}+1,r_{N^{\prime}+1}}}^{j_{r_{N^{\prime}+1}}}

for some
g_{j}\in\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z},d}(s-(2j_{0}+j_{1}+\dots+j_{r_{N^{\prime}+1}}))
. For all
w\geq 1
, denote

G_{w}:=\sum_{j=(j_{0},\dots,j_{r_{N^{\prime}+1}})\in\mathbb{N}^{r_{N^{\prime}+1}+1}\colon 2j_{0}+j_{1}+\dots+j_{w}\leq s,j_{w+1}=\dots=j_{r_{N^{\prime}}+1}=0,|j|\leq z}g_{j}\tilde{M}^{j_{0}}\prod_{t=1}^{w}L_{h_{N^{\prime}+1,t}}^{j_{t}}.

It follows from (
3.4
) that we may write
f
as

(3.5)

f=\sum_{i=(i_{1},\dots,i_{m_{N^{\prime}+1}})\in\mathbb{N}^{m_{N^{\prime}+1}}\colon w_{N^{\prime}+1}(i)\leq s,|i|\leq z}g^{\prime}_{i}\prod_{t=1}^{m_{N^{\prime}+1}}F_{N^{\prime}+1,t}^{i_{t}}+G_{r_{N^{\prime}+1}}

for some
g^{\prime}_{i}\in\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z},d}(s-w_{N^{\prime}+1}(i))
(in fact we can simply take
g^{\prime}_{i}=0
). In other words,

(3.6)

f-G_{r_{N^{\prime}+1}}\in\mathcal{B}_{N^{\prime}+1,z}.

Note that for every
F\in\mathcal{A}_{N^{\prime}+1}
there exist an integer coefficient homogeneous polynomial
R
and some
F^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{N^{\prime}}
such that
F=RF^{\prime}
. So combining (
3.3
) and (
3.5
), we have that

(3.7)

\sum_{i=(i_{1},\dots,i_{m_{N^{\prime}}})\in\mathbb{N}^{m_{N^{\prime}}}\colon w_{N^{\prime}}(i)\leq s,|i|\leq z}g^{\prime\prime}_{i}\prod_{t=1}^{m_{N^{\prime}}}F_{N^{\prime},t}^{i_{t}}+G_{r_{N^{\prime}+1}}=0

for some
g^{\prime\prime}_{i}\in\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z},d}(s-w_{N^{\prime}}(i))
.
Let
V^{\prime}=\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{h_{N^{\prime}+1,1},\dots,h_{N^{\prime}+1,r_{N^{\prime}+1}-1}\}.

Then it follows from (
3.7
) that

(3.8)

0\equiv G_{r_{N^{\prime}+1}}\equiv G_{r_{N^{\prime}+1}}-G_{r_{N^{\prime}+1}-1}\mod\cap_{i=1}^{N^{\prime}}J^{M}_{V+V^{\prime}+V_{i}}.

Note that we may write

G_{r_{N^{\prime}+1}}-G_{r_{N^{\prime}+1}-1}=L_{h_{N^{\prime}+1},r_{N^{\prime}+1}}G^{\prime}

for some
G^{\prime}\in\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z}/p^{z-1},d}(s-1)
.
Since

V,V_{1},\dots,V_{N^{\prime}+1}
are linearly independent,

(3.9)

the vectors
h_{\ell},h_{i,j},1\leq\ell\leq m,1\leq i\leq N^{\prime}+1,1\leq j\leq r_{i}
are linearly independent.

Since
\text{rank}(M|_{V^{\perp_{M}}})\geq 2(N+1)(r-1)+7\geq 4r+3
and
\dim(V^{\prime}+V_{i}+\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{h_{N^{\prime}+1,r_{N^{\prime}+1}}\})\leq 2r
for all
1\leq i\leq N^{\prime}
,
by Lemma
A.2
, we have that

\text{rank}(M|_{(V+V^{\prime}+V_{i}+\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{h_{N^{\prime}+1,r_{N^{\prime}+1}}\})^{\perp_{M}}})\geq 3

for all
1\leq i\leq N^{\prime}
.
Therefore, it follows from (
3.8
) and Lemma
3.8
that

\frac{1}{p}G^{\prime}\in\cap_{i=1}^{N^{\prime}}J^{M}_{V+V^{\prime}+V_{i}}.

Since
\text{rank}(M|_{V^{\perp_{M}}})\geq 2(N+1)(r-1)+7
, we have that
\text{rank}(M|_{(V+V^{\prime})^{\perp_{M}}})\geq 2N(r-1)+7
by Lemma
A.2
.
By (
3.9
),
V+V^{\prime},V_{1},\dots,V_{N}
are linearly independent. Applying the induction hypothesis for
V+V^{\prime},V_{1},\dots,V_{N^{\prime}}
, we may write
\frac{1}{p}G^{\prime}
as

(3.10)

\frac{1}{p}G^{\prime}=\sum_{i=(i_{1},\dots,i_{m_{\ast}})\in\mathbb{N}^{m_{\ast}}\colon w_{\ast}(i)\leq s,|i|\leq z}G^{\prime}_{i}\prod_{t=1}^{m_{\ast}}\tilde{F}_{t}^{i_{t}}

for some
G^{\prime}_{i}\in\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z},d}(s-w_{\ast}(i))
, where
\{\tilde{F}_{1},\dots,\tilde{F}_{m_{\ast}}\}
is an enumeration of the set

\tilde{\mathcal{A}}:=\Bigl\{\tilde{M},L_{h_{1}},\dots,L_{h_{m}},L_{h_{N^{\prime}+1},1},\dots,L_{h_{N^{\prime}+1},r_{N^{\prime}+1}-1},p^{N^{\prime}-1}\prod_{i=1}^{N^{\prime}}L_{h_{i,j_{i}}}\colon 1\leq j_{1},\dots,j_{t}\leq r_{i}\Bigr\}

and
w_{\ast}(i):=\sum_{t=1}^{m_{\ast}}\deg(\tilde{F}_{t})i_{t}
.
So it follows from (
3.10
) that

G_{r_{N^{\prime}+1}}-G_{r_{N^{\prime}+1}-1}=\sum_{i=(i_{1},\dots,i_{m_{\ast}})\in\mathbb{N}^{m_{\ast}}\colon w_{\ast}(i)\leq s,|i|\leq z}G^{\prime}_{i}(pL_{h_{N^{\prime}+1},r_{N^{\prime}+1}})\prod_{t=1}^{m_{\ast}}\tilde{F}_{t}^{i_{t}}.

Note that for every
F\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
, there exist an integer coefficient homogeneous polynomial
R
and some
F^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{N+1}
such that
F^{\prime}R=(pL_{h_{N^{\prime}+1},r_{N^{\prime}+1}})F
.
Therefore, we have that
G_{r_{N^{\prime}+1}}-G_{r_{N^{\prime}+1}-1}\in\mathcal{B}_{N^{\prime}+1,z}
. It then follows from (
3.6
) that

(3.11)

f-G_{r_{N^{\prime}+1}-1}\in\mathcal{B}_{N^{\prime}+1,z}.

Comparing (
3.3
) and (
3.11
), we have that

(3.12)

\sum_{i=(i_{1},\dots,i_{m_{N^{\prime}}})\in\mathbb{N}^{m_{N^{\prime}}}\colon w_{N^{\prime}}(i)\leq s,|i|\leq z}g^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i}\prod_{t=1}^{m_{N^{\prime}}}F_{N^{\prime},t}^{i_{t}}+G_{r_{N^{\prime}+1}-1}=0

for some
g^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i}\in\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z},d}(s-w_{N^{\prime}}(i))
.
The differences between (
3.6
), (
3.7
) and (
3.11
), (
3.12
) is that we replaced
G_{r_{N^{\prime}+1}}
by
G_{r_{N^{\prime}+1}-1}
, which removes all the terms in
G_{r_{N^{\prime}+1}}
having
L_{h_{N^{\prime}+1},r_{N^{\prime}+1}}
as a factor.
So we may continue this argument to replace
G_{r_{N^{\prime}+1}-1}
by
G_{r_{N^{\prime}+1}-2}
and eventually show that
f\in\mathcal{B}_{N^{\prime}+1,z}
.

The “in particular” part can be proved directly by unpacking the definition of
\mathcal{B}_{N^{\prime},z}
and we omit the details.
∎

We are now ready to prove Proposition
3.7
.

Proof of Proposition
3.7
.

The
\Leftarrow
direction is straightforward, and so we only need to prove the
\Rightarrow
direction.

Recall that
h_{1},\dots,h_{m}
be a basis of
V
.
Since
f\in\cap_{i=1}^{s+1}J^{M}_{V+V_{i}}
,
by Proposition
3.9
, if

p\gg_{d}1
, then

f\in\mathcal{B}_{N,z}
for some
z\in\mathbb{N}
and so we may write
f
as

\sum_{i=(i_{1},\dots,i_{m_{N}})\in\mathbb{N}^{m_{N}}\colon w_{N}(i)\leq s}R_{i}\prod_{i=1}^{m_{N}}\tilde{M}^{i_{0}}F_{N,i}^{i_{j}}

for some
R_{i}\in\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z},d}(s-w_{N}(i))
.
Since
f\in\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z}/p^{\mathbb{N}},d}(s)
and since
N\geq s+1
,
we may assume that
R_{i}=0
for all
F_{N,i}
whose degree is greater than
N
. Then the only remaining terms
F_{N,i}
are
\tilde{M},L_{h_{1}},\dots,L_{h_{m}}
. This implies that

f\in J^{M}_{V}

and we are done.
∎

We conclude this section with a density version of Proposition
3.6
:

Proposition 3.10

(Intersection property for large collection of
M
-modules)
.

Let
m,k,s\in\mathbb{N}
,
d,d^{\prime},K\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
\delta>0
,
p\gg_{\delta,d,k}1
be a prime dividing
K
,
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a non-degenerate quadratic form,
V,U_{1},\dots,U_{k}
be subspaces of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
such that all of
U_{1},\dots,U_{k}
have the same dimension and that
(U_{i},V)
is a linearly independent pair for all
1\leq i\leq k
, and
P\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}
be a subset with
|P|>\delta K^{d^{\prime}}
. For convenience denote
U_{0}=\{\mathbb{0}\}
.
Let
f\in\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z}/p^{\mathbb{N}},d}(s)
be such that

f\in J^{M}_{\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\iota(h_{1}),\dots,\iota(h_{m})\}+V}
for all
h_{1},\dots,h_{m}\in P
with
(h_{1},\dots,h_{m},U_{i},V),0\leq i\leq k
being linearly independent tuples.
9
9

9

We remark that the introduction to the notation
U_{0}
is redundant if
k\geq 1
, as the linear independence of
(x,y,U_{1},V)
implies that of
(x,y,V)
and
(x,y,U_{0},V)
. We introduce the notation
U_{0}
in order to combine the results for the case
k=0
and the case
k\geq 1
.

If
d^{\prime}\geq\max\{\dim(V)+m+s,\dim(U_{k})+\dim(V)+m,2\dim(V)+2m+5\}
, then we have that
f\in J^{M}_{V}.

Proof.

Our strategy is to use the largeness of the set
P
to iteratively find subspaces of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
satisfying the requirement of Proposition
3.6
.
Let
P_{1}
denote the set of
h_{1}\in P
such that
(h_{1},U_{i},V),0\leq i\leq k
are linearly independent tuples.
For
2\leq j\leq m
and
h_{1},\dots,h_{j-1}\in P
such that
(h_{1},\dots,h_{j-1},U_{i},V),0\leq i\leq k
are linearly independent tuples, let

P_{j}(h_{1},\dots,h_{j-1})
denote the set of
h_{j}\in P
such that
(h_{1},\dots,h_{j},U_{i},V),0\leq i\leq k
are linearly independent tuples.
By assumption, we have that

(3.13)

f\in\cap_{h_{1}\in P_{1}}\cap_{h_{2}\in P_{2}(h_{1})}\dots\cap_{h_{m}\in P_{m}(h_{1},\dots,h_{m-1})}J^{M}_{\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\iota(h_{1}),\dots,\iota(h_{m})\}+V}.

For
1\leq j\leq m
, we say that
Property-
j
holds if

f\in\cap_{h_{1}\in P_{1}}\cap_{h_{2}\in P_{2}(h_{1})}\dots\cap_{h_{j}\in P_{j}(h_{1},\dots,h_{j-1})}J^{M}_{\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\iota(h_{1}),\dots,\iota(h_{j-1})\}+V}.

We say that
Property-0
holds if
f\in J^{M}_{V}
.
By (
3.13
), we have that Property-
m
holds. Suppose that we have shown that Property-
(j+1)
holds for some
0\leq j\leq m-1
. Then

f\in\cap_{h_{1}\in P_{1}}\cap_{h_{2}\in P_{2}(h_{1})}\dots\cap_{h_{j+1}\in P_{j+1}(h_{1},\dots,h_{j})}J^{M}_{\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\iota(h_{1}),\dots,\iota(h_{j+1})\}+V}.

Fix any
h_{1},\dots,h_{j}\in P
such that
(h_{1},\dots,h_{j},U_{i},V),0\leq i\leq k
are linearly independent tuples.
Since
d^{\prime}\geq\max\{\dim(V)+m+s,\dim(U_{k})+\dim(V)+m\}
, it is not hard to find
p
-linearly independent
x_{1},\dots,x_{\dim(V)+j+s+1}\in P
such that
(h_{1},\dots,h_{j},x_{j^{\prime}},U_{i},V),0\leq i\leq k,1\leq j^{\prime}\leq\dim(V)+j+s+1
are linearly independent tuples.
Since Property-
(j+1)
holds, we have that

f\in\cap_{i=1}^{\dim(V)+j+s+1}J^{M}_{\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\iota(h_{1}),\dots,\iota(h_{m}),\iota(x_{i})\}+V}.

Since
d^{\prime}\geq 2\dim(V)+2m+5
,
applying Proposition
3.6
for
r=1
and
m=\dim(V)+j
, we have that
f\in J^{M}_{\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\iota(h_{1}),\dots,\iota(h_{j})\}+V}
. Since
h_{1},\dots,h_{j}
are arbitrary, this implies that Property-
j
holds.

Inductively, we conclude that Property-0 holds, meaning that
f\in J^{M}_{V}
.
∎

4.
Freiman equations for
M
-modules

4.1.
Solutions to Freiman equations

In additive combinatorics, subsets of a discrete abelian group with small doubling constant has a strong connection with generalized arithmetic progressions and locally linear functions, which we recall as follows:

Definition 4.1

(Generalized arithmetic progression)
.

Let
G
be an abelian group.
We say that
P\subseteq G
is a
generalized arithmetic progression
in
G
if
P
can be written as

(4.1)

P=\{a+\ell_{1}v_{1}+\dots+\ell_{D}v_{D}\colon\ell_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}\cap(-L_{i},L_{i})\text{ for all }1\leq i\leq D\}

for some
D\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
a,v_{1},\dots,v_{D}\in G
and
L_{1},\dots,L_{D}>0
. We abbreviate (
4.1
) as
P=a+(-L,L)\cdot v
, where
L:=(L_{1},\dots,L_{D})
and
v:=(v_{1},\dots,v_{D})
. We say that
a
is the
base point
,
D
the
rank
,
v_{1},\dots,v_{D}
the
generators
, and
L_{1},\dots,L_{D}
the
lengths
of
P
. If all sums in
P
are district,
then we say that
P
is
proper
. If
a=id_{G}
, then we say that
P
is
homogeneous
.

Definition 4.2

(Locally linear function)
.

Let
G,G^{\prime}
be abelian groups
and

P\subseteq G
be a proper and homogeneous generalized arithmetic progression given by (
4.1
). We say that a map
\xi
from
P
to
G^{\prime}
is
locally linear
if there exist
f_{0},f_{1},\dots,f_{D}
in
G^{\prime}
such that

\xi(\ell_{1}v_{1}+\dots+\ell_{D}v_{D})=f_{0}+\ell_{1}f_{1}+\dots+\ell_{D}f_{D}

for all
\ell_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}\cap(-L_{i},L_{i}),1\leq i\leq D
.

In this section, we focus on determine the solutions to Freiman equations. For abelian groups, the
Freiman equation
is of the form

(4.2)

F(h_{1})+F(h_{2})\equiv F(h_{3})+F(h_{4})\mod\mathbb{Z}\text{ for all }h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4}\in P\text{ with }h_{1}+h_{2}=h_{3}+h_{4},

where
P\subseteq G
is a proper and homogeneous generalized arithmetic progression in
G
and
F\colon G\to\mathbb{R}
is a function. Assume that
P
is given by (
4.1
). Then it is not hard to see that the value of
F
on
P
is determined by
F(\mathbb{0}),F(v_{i}),1\leq i\leq D
, and thus
F
is a locally linear function.

In this paper, we need to understand the solutions to the Freiman equation for shifted modules.
Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
H
be a subset of
\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}
.
We say that a map
\xi\colon H\to\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z}/p^{\mathbb{N}},d}(s)
is a
Freiman
M
-homomorphism of order
2^{n}
on
H
if for all
h_{1},\dots,h_{2^{n}}\in H
with
h_{1}+\dots+h_{2^{n-1}}=h_{2^{n-1}+1}+\dots+h_{2^{n}}
, we have that

(4.3)

\xi(h_{1})+\dots+\xi(h_{2^{n-1}})\equiv\xi(h_{2^{n-1}+1})+\dots+\xi(h_{2^{n}})\mod J^{M}_{\iota(h_{1}),\dots,\iota(h_{2^{n}-1})}.

In particular,
\xi
is a Freiman
M
-homomorphism of order 4 (on
H
) if

(4.4)

\xi(h_{1})+\xi(h_{2})\equiv\xi(h_{3})+\xi(h_{4})\mod J^{M}_{\iota(h_{1}),\iota(h_{2}),\iota(h_{3})}

for all
h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4}\in H
with
h_{1}+h_{2}=h_{3}+h_{4}
.

10
10

10

We remark that Freiman
M
-homomorphisms of order 4 is not the same as Freiman homomorphisms introduced in Definition
1.1
, since we are allowed to modulo
J^{M}_{\iota(h_{1}),\dots,\iota(h_{2^{n}-1})}
for Freiman
M
-homomorphisms.

Clearly, every locally linear map is a Freiman
M
-homomorphism of order 4.
The main result of this section shows that the converse also holds:
any Freiman
M
-homomorphism of order 4 is also locally linear when restricted to an appropriate sub generalized arithmetic progression if the dimension
d
is large.
For
0<c\leq 1
, let
P(c)
denote the generalized arithmetic progression

P(c)=\{\ell_{1}v_{1}+\dots+\ell_{D}v_{D}\colon\ell_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}\cap(-cL_{i},cL_{i}),1\leq i\leq D\}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}.

Since

\frac{|\mathbb{Z}\cap(-cL,cL)|}{|\mathbb{Z}\cap(-L,L)|}\geq\frac{\max\{2cL-1,1\}}{2L+1}\geq\frac{2cL-1}{2L+1}\cdot\mathbb{1}_{L>1/c}+\frac{1}{2L+1}\cdot\mathbb{1}_{L\leq 1/c}\geq\frac{c}{c+2}

for all
L>0
and
0<c<1
. We have that

(4.5)

|P(c)|\geq(\frac{c}{c+2})^{D}|P|.

The task of this section is to solve the Freiman equations for
M
-modules:

Theorem 4.3

(Solution to Freiman equations I)
.

Let
d,D,K\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
r,s\in\mathbb{N}
with
d\geq\max\{s+10,11\}
,
12
12

12

With a little more effort, one can release the restriction to
d\geq s+10
by studying the case
s=0
more carefully. We leave the details to interested readers.

\delta>0
,
p\gg_{\delta,d,D,r}1
be a prime dividing
K
, and
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a non-degenerate quadratic form. Let
P\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}
be a proper and homogeneous generalized arithmetic progression of dimension
D
with
|P|>\delta K^{d}
.
If
\xi\colon P\to\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z}/p^{r},d}(s)
is a Freiman
M
-homomorphism of order 4, then there exist a constant
0<c\leq 1
depending only on
r,s

and a locally linear map
T\colon P(c)\to\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z}/p^{r},d}(s)
such that

\xi(h)\equiv T(h)\mod J^{M}_{\iota(h)}
for all
h\in P(c)
.
13
13

13

It is natural to ask that whether the conclusion Theorem
4.3
holds for all
h\in P
instead of for
h\in P(c)
. Such as an improvement seems promising by applying another bootstrap argument on Theorem
4.3
, but in this paper we do not need such a general result.

Remark 4.4
.

Note that Theorem
4.7
fails if the dimension
d
is small. In particular, Theorem
4.7
fails when
s=1
and
\dim(P)=d=2
. Indeed, let
\xi\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{2}
be given by
\xi(x,y)=xf(yx^{-1})
for all
x,y\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{2},x\neq 0
and
\xi(0,y)=0
, where
f\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}\to\mathbb{F}^{2}_{p}
is arbitrary. Then it is not hard to see that (
4.4
) holds for all
h_{1},\dots,h_{4}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{2}
(in fact, if
d=2
, then (
4.4
) holds trivially when
h_{1},\dots,h_{4}
spans
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{2}
, and holds by the construction of
\xi
when the span of
h_{1},\dots,h_{4}
is of dimension 0 or 1). On the other hand, in general,
\xi
does not coincides with a locally linear map mod
J^{M}_{\iota(h)}
. So it is an interesting question to ask what the sharp lower bound of
d
is for the conclusion of Theorem
4.7
to hold.

4.2.
M
-modules in
\mathbb{F}_{p}[x_{1},\dots,x_{d}]

To prove Theorem
4.3
, our first step is to study the case
r=1
. Since any
\mathbb{Z}/p
-coefficient polynomial
f
in
\mathbb{R}[x_{1},\dots,x_{d}]
can be identified naturally as a polynomial in
\mathbb{F}_{p}[x_{1},\dots,x_{d}]
given by
\iota\circ(pf)\circ\tau
, it is convenient to extend the concept of
M
-modules to polynomials in
\mathbb{F}_{p}[x_{1},\dots,x_{d}]
.
Let
\text{HP}_{d}(s)
denote the set of homogeneous polynomial in
\mathbb{F}_{p}[x_{1},\dots,x_{d}]
of degree
s
. We adopt a convention similar to Convention
1.3
.

Definition 4.5

(
M
-module for
\mathbb{F}_{p}
polynomials)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+},p
be a prime and
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form associated with the matrix
A
(see Section
1.2
for definitions).
We say that a subset
I
of the polynomial ring
\mathbb{F}_{p}[x_{1},\dots,x_{d}]

is an
M
-module
if there exists a subspace
V
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
such that

f\in I\Leftrightarrow f(n)=0\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\text{ with }M(n)=0\text{ and }(hA)\cdot n=0\text{ for all }h\in V

(or equivalently,
f\in I\Leftrightarrow f(n)=0\text{ for all }n\in V(M)\cap V^{\perp_{M}}
).
In this case we denote
I
as
J^{M}_{V}
. For convenience we denote

J^{M}:=J^{M}_{\{\mathbb{0}\}}\text{ and }J^{M}_{h_{1},\dots,h_{k}}:=J^{M}_{\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{h_{1},\dots,h_{k}\}}.

for all
h_{1},\dots,h_{k}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.

Remark 4.6
.

To simplify notations, we use the same notation
J^{M}_{V}
in Definitions
1.4
and
4.5
to denote
M
-modules over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
and over
\mathbb{R}
. This will not cause confusions as the meaning of
J^{M}_{V}
will be clear from the context.

Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
H
be a subset of
\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}
.
We say that a map
\xi\colon H\to\text{HP}_{d}(s)
is a
Freiman
M
-homomorphism of order
2^{n}
on
H
if (
4.3
) holds (in the sense of Definition
4.5
) for all
h_{1},\dots,h_{2^{n}}\in H
with
h_{1}+\dots+h_{2^{n-1}}=h_{2^{n-1}+1}+\dots+h_{2^{n}}
.
A key step in proving Theorem
4.3
is to solve the Freiman equations for
M
-modules in
\mathbb{F}_{p}[x_{1},\dots,x_{d}]
.

Theorem 4.7

(Solution to Freiman equations II)
.

Let
d,D,K\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
s\in\mathbb{N}
with
d\geq\max\{s+10,11\}
,
\delta>0
,
p\gg_{\delta,d,D}1
be a prime dividing
K
, and
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a non-degenerate quadratic form. Let
P\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}
be a proper and homogeneous generalized arithmetic progression of dimension
D
with
|P|>\delta K^{d}
.
If
\xi\colon P\to\text{HP}_{d}(s)
is a Freiman
M
-homomorphism of order 4, then there exist a constant
0<c\leq 1
depending only on
s

and a locally linear map
T\colon P(c)\to\text{HP}_{d}(s)
such that

\xi(h)\equiv T(h)\mod J^{M}_{\iota(h)}
for all
h\in P(c)
.

Since one can identify
\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z}/p,d}(s)
with
\text{HP}_{d}(s)
, it is not hard to see that Theorem
4.7
is the special case of Theorem
4.3
for
r=1
. We will first prove Theorem
4.7
in
Sections
4.3
to
4.6
. Then in Section
4.7
, we prove Theorem
4.3
by using Theorem
4.7
and the
p
-expansion trick introduced in
[
7
]
.

Before proving Theorem
4.7
, we summarize some useful properties for
M
-modules in
\mathbb{F}_{p}[x_{1},\dots,x_{d}]
.
In the rest of this section, for
x,n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, denote
L_{n}(x):=(nA)\cdot x
, where
A
is the matrix associated to
M
.
If
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}
, then we denote
L_{n}:=L_{\iota(n)}
for convenience. By identifying
\text{HP}_{\mathbb{Z}/p,d}(s)
with
\text{HP}_{d}(s)
, we immediate deduce the following results:

Lemma 4.8

(A special case of Lemma
3.8
)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
k,s\in\mathbb{N}
,
p\gg_{d,k,s}1
be a prime,
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a non-degenerate quadratic form associated with the matrix
A
, and
h,h_{1},\dots,h_{k}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
be linearly independent vectors. Let
f\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a polynomial of degree
s
such that
L_{h}f\in J^{M}_{h_{1},\dots,h_{k}}
. If

\text{rank}(M|_{\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{h,h_{1},\dots,h_{k}\}^{\perp_{M}}})\geq 3
or
d\geq 2k+5
, then we have that
f\in J^{M}_{h_{1},\dots,h_{k}}
.

Proposition 4.9

(A special case of Proposition
3.10
)
.

Let
m,k,s\in\mathbb{N}
,
d,d^{\prime},K\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
\delta>0
,
p\gg_{\delta,d,k}1
be a prime dividing
K
,
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a non-degenerate quadratic form,
V,U_{1},\dots,U_{k}
be subspaces of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
such that all of
U_{1},\dots,U_{k}
have the same dimension and that
(U_{i},V)
is a linearly independent pair for all
1\leq i\leq k
, and
P\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}
be a subset with
|P|>\delta K^{d^{\prime}}
. For convenience denote
U_{0}=\{\mathbb{0}\}
.
Let
f\in\text{HP}_{d}(s)
be such that

f\in J^{M}_{\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\iota(h_{1}),\dots,\iota(h_{m})\}+V}
for all
h_{1},\dots,h_{m}\in P
with
(h_{1},\dots,h_{m},U_{i},V),0\leq i\leq k
being linearly independent tuples. If
d^{\prime}\geq\max\{\dim(V)+m+s,\dim(U_{k})+\dim(V)+m,2\dim(V)+2m+5\}
, then we have that
f\in J^{M}_{V}.

Proposition 4.10

(A special case of Proposition
A.8
)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
k,s\in\mathbb{N}
,
p\gg_{d,k,s}1
be a prime number,

M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a non-degenerate quadratic form, and
V
be a subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
of dimension
k
with a basis
h_{1},\dots,h_{k}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
. Suppose that
\text{rank}(M|_{V^{\perp_{M}}})\geq 3
. Then for all
f\in\text{HP}_{d}(s)
, we have that
f\in J^{M}_{V}
if and only if

f=R_{0}M_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}R_{i}L_{h_{i}}

for some
R_{0}\in\text{HP}_{d}(s-2)
and
R_{i}\in\text{HP}_{d}(s-1)
,
where
M_{0}(n):=(nA)\cdot n
.
In particular, the conclusion of this proposition holds if
d\geq k+\dim(V\cap V^{\perp_{M}})+3
, or if
d\geq 2k+3
.

4.3.
Solutions to some fundamental equations

The proof of Theorem
4.7
is rather intricate.
Unlike in the abelian setting, for Theorem
4.7
, the values of
\xi
at
\mathbb{0},v_{i},1\leq i\leq D
are far from being enough to determine the entire function
\xi
.
In order to prove Theorem
4.7
, we begin with solving some (relatively) simple equations. Then we gradually increase the complexity of the equations until we reach (
4.4
).

We start with the following trivial equation:

(4.6)

f(x)\equiv f(y)\mod\mathbb{Z}\text{ for all $x,y\in G$},

where
f
is a map from
G
to
\mathbb{R}
for some group
G
. It is obvious that the solution to (
4.6
) is
f\equiv c\mod\mathbb{Z}
for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
.
In the setting of shifted modules, one can study a similar equation:

(4.7)

F(x)\equiv F(y)\mod J^{M}_{\iota(x),\iota(y)}\text{ for all $x,y\in\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}$},

where
F
is a map from
\mathbb{Z}_{K}^{d}
to
\text{HP}_{d}(s)
. Comparing (
4.6
) with (
4.7
), it is natural to conjecture that the solution to (
4.6
) is
F(x)\equiv f\mod J^{M}_{\iota(x)}
for some
f\in\text{HP}_{d}(s)
. This is indeed the case at least when
\iota(x)\neq\mathbb{0}
.

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
d,K\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
s\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
p\gg_{d,s}1
be a prime dividing
K
.
Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
